Mode contract

recorded (outcome.json contract field)

Verbatim, as recorded with the run and sent to the agents.

Hard-block primitive to instantiate: `mult_add_int_18x19`   (precision INT19)
  computes: out = ax*ay + bx
  input ports:  clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[36], chainin[64], scanin[19]
  output ports: result[64], chainout[64], scanout[19]
  arithmetic: the block's operands and result are signed two's complement. A design operand narrower than its port must be widened to the port width: zero-extended if the design treats it as unsigned, sign-extended if signed. An unsigned design operand as wide as its port has no exact representation on this block.
  timing: `result` is registered inside the block on `clk`; it is valid 2 cycles after the input operands are applied.
  registers: the block registers its operands (`ax`, `ay`, `bx`) at one clock edge and `result` at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers. Operands applied before clock edge k appear in `result` after edge k+1. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register.
  reset: `reset` is synchronous and active high; it clears the block's operand registers and its result register to 0.
  tie-off: `chainin` = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's `chainout` or a constant may drive it, and it is added to the sum at the result register, one clock edge before `result`); `scanin` = 0 always (the operand scan chain is not used: `ay` is driven directly, and `scanout` is left unconnected); `mode_sigs` = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
  Wire EVERY operand of the fused region onto the input ports (name them exactly as above); drive the region's real consumer from the output port. Tie unused term ports to 0. Do not leave input ports unconnected.

An id names a product by its module and its multiply. One that ends in (sum_in=<module>) is a product whose sum sits in that other module: the product leaves its own module through a port and is added there, so the arithmetic to convert spans both. A #1, #2 after an id separates instances of the same product that sit in sums of different shapes.

Baseline: the unmodified dla_like.small under this run's VPR settings (regime 'koios_small': channel width 300, device koios_small, seed 1) has a critical path of 6.81328 ns and uses 114 DSP tiles of the device's 306, one block per tile. Its used logic-block area is 1.67592e+08 minimum-width transistor areas (one DSP tile 253779, one cluster 27905), so its area-delay product is 1.14185e+09. Its slowest stage is in dsp_block_16_8_false: one hard multiply on a DSP tile (two_mult_18x19), then one adder chain of 12 carry stages, then one LUT, into a flip-flop with synchronous clear (resulta_tmp); wire 1.73 ns into the multiply, 0.96 into the adder, 1.39 into the LUT. The next slowest stage, in a different dsp_block_16_8_false instance (endpoint group $sdff~5296, not path 1's $sdff~4939), is the same shape as path 1 — one hard multiply on a DSP tile, one adder chain of 15 carry stages, one LUT, into the resulta_tmp flip-flop, 6.808 ns, so shortening the slowest stage past that point buys nothing on its own. Any path your rewrite creates is compared against that critical path.

Rewrite rules

A stage is the logic between one register and the next. A block is one instance of the target mode. These rules hold for any synchronous design on any FPGA. Numbers that depend on this architecture are in the last section; use them, do not restate them as rules.

Must hold

Going against these produces a wrong design, not a slower one.

Observability. Only a block's output is visible. A value read by anything other than the arithmetic being taken into the block (another expression, a module output port, another module) cannot go inside a block. Keep it outside, or recompute it for those readers. Check the source; detection does not see port consumers. Operands are exempt: the block takes a copy and the original signal still exists.

Register control. A register carries its reset, enable and initial value. These modes hold no registers, so taking a register inside deletes that behaviour. If anything depends on it, keep the register and drive it from the block's result, or leave the site alone.

Arithmetic. The block computes the mode's expression on signed ports. Match the original bit for bit or leave the site alone. Keep every term of the original expression, including ports every instantiation ties to a constant. A term that goes on no block port (an addend beside the products) stays outside the block, added to its result; the flow flattens the design and removes an add of a constant, so such a term puts nothing on the stage and rule 2 does not count it. A bound on a port's values may be used only if the plan states it and names its source in the design, on a line of its own beginning ASSUMES:; such a rewrite is recorded as verified under that assumption. Fold a term only when it is constant inside the module itself, and say so in the plan.

design operandon a signed portallowed
unsigned, narrower than the portzero-extendyes
unsigned, as wide as the port, range provably below the signed maximum (constant coefficient, bounded value)zero-extend, state the bound and where it comes fromyes
unsigned, as wide as the port, range not boundable from the source (a top-level input)no exact representationno
original truncates each product before the suma fused sum truncated after differsno
original's narrow sum register wrapsthe low bits of the wider result reproduce the wrapyes
Weigh

The clock period is the delay of the slowest stage in the whole design. A stage's delay is the sum of everything on it: each block's fixed delay, each wire, each soft cell. Each relationship below names what it does to delay and to area. If a plan goes against one, say why.

1. Slowest stage → all outputs

Your task names the design's slowest stage and what is on it.

Slowest stage → delay

Relationship: A rewrite that does not touch the slowest stage cannot make the design faster, and must leave every stage no slower than it was. A rewrite that does touch it must make it shorter, not merely different. Compare every stage you change against it.

Slowest stage → area

Relationship: A rewrite that cannot shorten the clock period is paid for in tiles alone. Say so in the plan.

2. Block to register → delay

Relationship: A block's result goes to a register, or into the next block, with nothing but wire between. Any soft arithmetic between a block and its register is added to the stage.

3. Blocks in series → all outputs
Blocks in series → delay

Relationship: Each block in series adds its own delay plus one wire. Sum them with the wire in and the wire out, and compare against the slowest stage. That comparison, not a fixed count, says how many fit.

Blocks in series → area

Relationship: None. Series or side by side, the same blocks take the same tiles.

4. Arithmetic inside the block → all outputs

Arithmetic inside the block is what the mode's expression computes rather than what sits in series with it: a multiply and one addend, or two products and their sum, depending on the mode.

Arithmetic inside the block → delay

Relationship: Free. The block's delay is the mode's own and does not rise because its adder is used. This is where a conversion wins: soft logic leaves the stage and the block's delay already covers the work. An adder feeding the block's addend port is mapped into the block; an adder reading the block's result is on the path after it (rule 2). A block whose addend port is tied to a constant takes in nothing; if the multiply it replaces is already on a tile, the conversion changes the tile count and nothing else.

Arithmetic inside the block → area

Relationship: Flip-flops fall where product registers go. Do not count on LUT savings.

5. Registers → all outputs
Registers → delay

Relationship: A register may move only if no stage becomes longer and the module's latency is unchanged. If one must be added to keep latency, put it where it splits the longest stage. Every other operand entering that stage must be delayed by the same amount, or the stage adds values from different cycles.

Registers → area

Relationship: A register placed mid-chain costs operand-delay registers that a register after the chain does not.

6. Tiles → area

Relationship: A block occupies one DSP tile in one mode. Do not assume the multiply it replaces frees a tile: the synthesis tool may already have placed that multiply on a tile shared with others. Count the blocks against the tiles the unmodified design uses and the device's count, both in your task.

Every DSP mode costs the same 2.14 ns. The invented modes have no chain ports, so a partial sum passes between blocks through general routing. The packer can place up to four multiplies of 9 bits or less on one tile and up to two of 18 bits or less; a block in mult_add_mode_18_19_36 takes its tile alone.

Before you commit to a plan

State in your plan: how many blocks and how many tiles on top of the unmodified design's; the longest series of blocks in any stage and its delay from the table, against the slowest stage; and what soft logic leaves the stage you touch, and whether that stage is the slowest one. If a site does not fit under those numbers, arrange it differently, take fewer, or leave it, and name the rule that ruled it out.

This mode's block holds registers

The rules above were written for blocks with no registers. This run's target mode is a registered block: its contract states a latency in cycles, where its registers sit, what its reset does and which ports are tied off. Read the rules above with the changes below.

Must hold

Register control, for this mode. The block has two register stages of its own: one on the operands, before the products, and one on the sum. A design register may go inside the block only as one of those two stages. A register on the operands, or on each product before the sum, is the first stage; the register that holds the sum is the second. The block's registers have the reset the contract states, no enable and no other initial value. A design register that has an enable, a different reset behaviour, or a reset signal other than the one you connect to the block's reset port cannot be replaced by the block's register: keep it outside or leave the site alone. Connect the block's reset to the signal that reset the registers it replaces, or to 0 if they had none.

Latency (rule 5, and it must hold). The module's latency must be unchanged: every output carries the same value on the same cycle as before. Count the register stages the original has from the operands to the register that holds the sum, and compare with the block's latency.

the original hasthen
as many stages as the blockthe block's registers stand in place of the design's; remove exactly those and move nothing else
morethe extra stage stays outside the block, on the operands before it or on the result after it, where it was
fewerthe block adds the difference: move a register that already exists on the same path into the block's place, or delay every other value that meets the block's result by the same number of cycles so that the stage adds values from the same cycle; if neither can be done without changing when the module's outputs change, leave the site alone

State in the plan the stages the original has around each site, which of them the block replaces, and the module's latency before and after. They must be equal.

Addends and the chain input. A term the original adds to the products before the sum's register cannot be added to the block's result on the same cycle: the result is already registered. The chain input is a dedicated pin that takes only another block's chain output or a constant. If the term is a module port that every instantiation ties to a constant, connect that port to the chain input: the block is then tied off exactly where the original was, and the module still computes what it computed for every value of its ports. Any other addend needs its own register (same reset) and an add after the block, which rule 2 counts on the next stage.

Weigh

Stages (rules 2 and 3). A registered block ends the stage that reaches its operand ports and starts a new one at its result. The wire into the block and the wire out of it are on different stages. Soft arithmetic after the block is on the stage after the block's result register, not in series with the block's delay. Between its own two registers the block takes its fixed delay from the table.

Flip-flops (rule 4). The design registers the block replaces leave the soft logic.

Objective: minimize the design's area-delay product, VPR's used area times the critical path, against the baseline in your task. A rewrite that does not lower it is not worth making; say so and output TIER: NONE rather than convert sites for their own sake. Among rewrites that lower it, prefer the one that lowers it most.

Classify the tier of the change you propose (a datatype/algorithm variant, T3, is out of scope —
do not propose one):

  • T1: Direct replacement: no change to the computed function or surrounding structure; no datatype change.
  • T2: Local restructuring only; preserve the region's function, role, and live consumers; no new operation/datatype family.

Read the source. Output EXACTLY this header (uppercase keys), then the plan:

Read these files in this directory: dla_like.small.v

response

I'll read the source first.

TIER: T2
REGIONS: dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1166, dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1167
SHARED_REDUCTION: resulta_tmp in dsp_block_16_8_false (dla_like.small.v:11745) — the single 24-bit sum ax_reg*ay_reg + bx_reg*by_reg + chainin, driving resulta → dot_product_16_8_30_2.o_result
BLOCKS: 144
TILES: 184
STAGE_NS: 3.37
MAPPING: per dot_product_16_8_30_2 instance, two chained blocks.
Block A (head): clk=clk; reset=i_reset (new port aclr_pre on dsp_block_16_8_false); ax={2'b0, ax[15:0]} (= i_features_0, unsigned 16 → zero-extend to 18); ay={11'b0, ay[7:0]} (= i_weights_0, unsigned 8 → zero-extend to 19); bx=36'd0; chainin= the module's chainin port (64'd0 at its only instantiation, dla_like.small.v:11687); mode_sigs=12'd0; scanin=19'd0; result unused; chainout → Block B's chainin; scanout unconnected.
Block B (tail): clk=clk; reset=aclr (= r_pipeline_0); ax={2'b0, bx[15:0]} (= f_pipeline_1_0); ay={11'b0, by[7:0]} (= w_pipeline_1_0); bx=36'd0; chainin=Block A's chainout; mode_sigs=12'd0; scanin=19'd0; result[23:0] → resulta (→ o_result), chainout of the module kept as {40'b0, result[23:0]}; scanout unconnected.
PLAN:

What is converted. dsp_block_16_8_false (one definition, 72 instances via dot_product_16_8_30_2) computes one registered sum of two products plus chainin — a genuine common reduction, and it is the design's slowest stage (and its second-slowest, same module, different instance). Both products of every instance are converted: 144 blocks.

Structure and latency. dot_product_16_8_30_2 has three register stages from i_features_* to o_result: f_pipeline_* (reset i_reset), ax_reg/ay_reg/bx_reg/by_reg (reset r_pipeline_0), resulta_tmp (reset r_pipeline_0). Each block supplies two of those stages, so the pair must be skewed by one cycle: Block A is fed combinationally from i_features_0/i_weights_0 — its operand register is f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0, which are deleted — and Block B is fed from the retained f_pipeline_1_0/w_pipeline_1_0. Input before edge e1 → A operands at e1 → A result at e2 → A chainout stable through the window before e3, where B adds it at its result register → o_result after e3. B: input before e1 → f_pipeline_1_0 at e1 → B operands at e2 → B result at e3. Both products come from the same input cycle and the module's latency is 3 edges before and 3 after; ax_reg…by_reg, resulta_tmp and the 24-bit soft adder are deleted. ena is unused in the baseline module, so no enable is lost.

Reset. B's two registers replace ax_reg…by_reg and resulta_tmp, both reset by r_pipeline_0 — an exact match. A's operand register replaces f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0, reset by i_reset, so A's reset is i_reset; that also clears A's result register one edge earlier than the original cleared ax_reg. That difference is unobservable: A's result is consumed only through B's chain input one edge later, and at that edge r_pipeline_0 is high, so B's result register is forced to 0 regardless. I walked the full reset/de-assert sequence (edges e1–e4) and o_result is identical cycle by cycle, including the stale-product value latched at the reset edge itself.

Exactness. ax,bx are 16-bit unsigned and ay,by 8-bit unsigned, each strictly narrower than its signed port, so zero-extension is exact and no value bound is needed (no ASSUMES: line). Each product is < 2^24 and nonnegative; the baseline evaluates the whole RHS in 64 bits (because chainin is 64-bit) and truncates to 24, so it does not truncate per product — the chained blocks also sum at full width and the low 24 bits reproduce the baseline's wrap. chainin is kept as a term on A's chain input, where the rules place a port every instantiation ties to a constant; being constant, its timing alignment is immaterial.

Consumers preserved. resulta → o_result → accumulator_24_30_3.i_result (dla_like.small.v:11494), which only sign-extends and registers it — wire into a register, nothing soft between block and register (rule 2). chainout/chains_0 is dangling at the only instantiation but is still driven identically. f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0 had no reader but the DSP instance, so deleting them hides nothing; i_features_0/i_weights_0 are operands (exempt) and are driven by registers (if_reg_* in the previous processing_element, pipeline0_reg_0 in the weight cache), so no combinational logic lands in front of A.

Delay. Longest series of blocks in any one stage: one. Stages created: register→A/B operands 0.06 + 2.25 = 2.31 (3.88 at the worst sampled wire); A operand reg→A result reg 0.06 + 2.14 = 2.20; A result reg→B result reg over the chain 0.06 + 1.17 + 2.14 = 3.37 (4.42 worst); B result→in_reg 0.06 + 1.48 ≈ 1.6. The 6.813 ns stage (multiply → 12-carry adder chain → LUT → resulta_tmp) and its 6.808 ns twin are both this module and both disappear, as do all 72 siblings of the same shape; the soft 24-bit adder chain and LUT leave the design entirely. The new period is then set by stages I do not touch — the deepest I found are single-level pipelined adds (inverse_winograd_adder_30_3 33-bit, winograd_adder_16_20_4 20-bit) and memory reads, all ≈2–3 ns.

Area. 144 blocks, one tile each (this mode takes its tile alone), displacing the 144 dot-product multiplies that today share 110 of the 114 tiles with the winograd multiplies; the ~80 winograd multiplies left behind repack two to a tile (~40 tiles) → ~184 tiles, upper bound 200 if all 112 winograd multiplies need DSPs, against 306 on the device. DSP area goes 2.89e7 → 4.67e7; cluster area drops slightly (72× a 24-bit adder chain plus ~96 flip-flops each move into the blocks), so total ≈1.85e8, about +11%. ADP improves as long as the new period is below ~6.16 ns, which the eliminated 6.81 ns stages and the ≤4.4 ns worst-case new stages make very likely.

Candidates left alone. All sixteen winograd_dsp_16.$mul$…$733/$734(sum_in=winograd_transform_0/1)#1–#4 regions (112 products) are left, for reasons that apply to all of them together: (1) truncation — resa_reg/resb_reg are 16-bit, so each 16×16 product is truncated to 16 bits before the sum, and winograd_adder_16_20_4 then zero-extends those 16 bits into a 20-bit add; a fused multiply-add truncated after the sum is not bit-identical, which the rules forbid outright; (2) observability — each dsp_out_* leaves winograd_dsp_16 as a module output read by several different winograd_adder_16_20_4 instances and by soft shift/negate expressions (e.g. dsp_out_1_5 feeds WA10 directly, WA20/WA50 negated, WA30 as >>>1, WA40 as -(>>>1)), so the product cannot move inside a block feeding one reduction; (3) latency — the product already uses both of the block's register stages (operand reg ay_reg, product reg resa_reg), while the sum it joins sits one further pipeline stage away inside the adder, so folding product and first add into one block needs three stages where the block offers two; and (4) cost without benefit — these multiplies are not on the slowest stage, they pair two to a tile today, and each conversion would take a tile alone, which rule 1 says is paid in tiles for no period gain.

Risks. The main one is the chain-timing reading: I take "chainin … added to the sum at the result register, one clock edge before result" to mean chainin is sampled in the same window in which the product is formed, i.e. the edge that latches result, and chainout to carry the block's registered result. The one-cycle operand skew between A and B follows from exactly that; if the chain input were instead sampled an edge earlier, the pair would need a two-cycle skew, which cannot be built here without raising dot_product_16_8_30_2's latency to 4 — in that case this site must be left alone. Secondary risks: the reset-signal split (A on i_reset, B on r_pipeline_0, requiring the new aclr_pre port on dsp_block_16_8_false, whose only instantiation is at dla_like.small.v:11679), the 70-tile increase, and the fact that the resulting period is set by stages outside this rewrite. No new operation or datatype is introduced.

Any path your rewrite creates is compared against that critical path.
The block's contract, its lines on timing, registers and tie-offs:
timing: result is registered inside the block on clk; it is valid 2 cycles after the input operands are applied.
registers: the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers. Operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register.
tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it, and it is added to the sum at the result register, one clock edge before result); scanin = 0 always (the operand scan chain is not used: ay is driven directly, and scanout is left unconnected); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).

I'll read the source first.

TIER: T2
REGIONS: dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1166, dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1167
SHARED_REDUCTION: resulta_tmp in dsp_block_16_8_false (dla_like.small.v:11745) — the single 24-bit sum ax_reg*ay_reg + bx_reg*by_reg + chainin, driving resulta → dot_product_16_8_30_2.o_result
BLOCKS: 144
TILES: 184
STAGE_NS: 3.37
MAPPING: per dot_product_16_8_30_2 instance, two chained blocks.
Block A (head): clk=clk; reset=i_reset (new port aclr_pre on dsp_block_16_8_false); ax={2'b0, ax[15:0]} (= i_features_0, unsigned 16 → zero-extend to 18); ay={11'b0, ay[7:0]} (= i_weights_0, unsigned 8 → zero-extend to 19); bx=36'd0; chainin= the module's chainin port (64'd0 at its only instantiation, dla_like.small.v:11687); mode_sigs=12'd0; scanin=19'd0; result unused; chainout → Block B's chainin; scanout unconnected.
Block B (tail): clk=clk; reset=aclr (= r_pipeline_0); ax={2'b0, bx[15:0]} (= f_pipeline_1_0); ay={11'b0, by[7:0]} (= w_pipeline_1_0); bx=36'd0; chainin=Block A's chainout; mode_sigs=12'd0; scanin=19'd0; result[23:0] → resulta (→ o_result), chainout of the module kept as {40'b0, result[23:0]}; scanout unconnected.
PLAN:

What is converted. dsp_block_16_8_false (one definition, 72 instances via dot_product_16_8_30_2) computes one registered sum of two products plus chainin — a genuine common reduction, and it is the design's slowest stage (and its second-slowest, same module, different instance). Both products of every instance are converted: 144 blocks.

Structure and latency. dot_product_16_8_30_2 has three register stages from i_features_* to o_result: f_pipeline_* (reset i_reset), ax_reg/ay_reg/bx_reg/by_reg (reset r_pipeline_0), resulta_tmp (reset r_pipeline_0). Each block supplies two of those stages, so the pair must be skewed by one cycle: Block A is fed combinationally from i_features_0/i_weights_0 — its operand register is f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0, which are deleted — and Block B is fed from the retained f_pipeline_1_0/w_pipeline_1_0. Input before edge e1 → A operands at e1 → A result at e2 → A chainout stable through the window before e3, where B adds it at its result register → o_result after e3. B: input before e1 → f_pipeline_1_0 at e1 → B operands at e2 → B result at e3. Both products come from the same input cycle and the module's latency is 3 edges before and 3 after; ax_reg…by_reg, resulta_tmp and the 24-bit soft adder are deleted. ena is unused in the baseline module, so no enable is lost.

Reset. B's two registers replace ax_reg…by_reg and resulta_tmp, both reset by r_pipeline_0 — an exact match. A's operand register replaces f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0, reset by i_reset, so A's reset is i_reset; that also clears A's result register one edge earlier than the original cleared ax_reg. That difference is unobservable: A's result is consumed only through B's chain input one edge later, and at that edge r_pipeline_0 is high, so B's result register is forced to 0 regardless. I walked the full reset/de-assert sequence (edges e1–e4) and o_result is identical cycle by cycle, including the stale-product value latched at the reset edge itself.

Exactness. ax,bx are 16-bit unsigned and ay,by 8-bit unsigned, each strictly narrower than its signed port, so zero-extension is exact and no value bound is needed (no ASSUMES: line). Each product is < 2^24 and nonnegative; the baseline evaluates the whole RHS in 64 bits (because chainin is 64-bit) and truncates to 24, so it does not truncate per product — the chained blocks also sum at full width and the low 24 bits reproduce the baseline's wrap. chainin is kept as a term on A's chain input, where the rules place a port every instantiation ties to a constant; being constant, its timing alignment is immaterial.

Consumers preserved. resulta → o_result → accumulator_24_30_3.i_result (dla_like.small.v:11494), which only sign-extends and registers it — wire into a register, nothing soft between block and register (rule 2). chainout/chains_0 is dangling at the only instantiation but is still driven identically. f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0 had no reader but the DSP instance, so deleting them hides nothing; i_features_0/i_weights_0 are operands (exempt) and are driven by registers (if_reg_* in the previous processing_element, pipeline0_reg_0 in the weight cache), so no combinational logic lands in front of A.

Delay. Longest series of blocks in any one stage: one. Stages created: register→A/B operands 0.06 + 2.25 = 2.31 (3.88 at the worst sampled wire); A operand reg→A result reg 0.06 + 2.14 = 2.20; A result reg→B result reg over the chain 0.06 + 1.17 + 2.14 = 3.37 (4.42 worst); B result→in_reg 0.06 + 1.48 ≈ 1.6. The 6.813 ns stage (multiply → 12-carry adder chain → LUT → resulta_tmp) and its 6.808 ns twin are both this module and both disappear, as do all 72 siblings of the same shape; the soft 24-bit adder chain and LUT leave the design entirely. The new period is then set by stages I do not touch — the deepest I found are single-level pipelined adds (inverse_winograd_adder_30_3 33-bit, winograd_adder_16_20_4 20-bit) and memory reads, all ≈2–3 ns.

Area. 144 blocks, one tile each (this mode takes its tile alone), displacing the 144 dot-product multiplies that today share 110 of the 114 tiles with the winograd multiplies; the ~80 winograd multiplies left behind repack two to a tile (~40 tiles) → ~184 tiles, upper bound 200 if all 112 winograd multiplies need DSPs, against 306 on the device. DSP area goes 2.89e7 → 4.67e7; cluster area drops slightly (72× a 24-bit adder chain plus ~96 flip-flops each move into the blocks), so total ≈1.85e8, about +11%. ADP improves as long as the new period is below ~6.16 ns, which the eliminated 6.81 ns stages and the ≤4.4 ns worst-case new stages make very likely.

Candidates left alone. All sixteen winograd_dsp_16.$mul$…$733/$734(sum_in=winograd_transform_0/1)#1–#4 regions (112 products) are left, for reasons that apply to all of them together: (1) truncation — resa_reg/resb_reg are 16-bit, so each 16×16 product is truncated to 16 bits before the sum, and winograd_adder_16_20_4 then zero-extends those 16 bits into a 20-bit add; a fused multiply-add truncated after the sum is not bit-identical, which the rules forbid outright; (2) observability — each dsp_out_* leaves winograd_dsp_16 as a module output read by several different winograd_adder_16_20_4 instances and by soft shift/negate expressions (e.g. dsp_out_1_5 feeds WA10 directly, WA20/WA50 negated, WA30 as >>>1, WA40 as -(>>>1)), so the product cannot move inside a block feeding one reduction; (3) latency — the product already uses both of the block's register stages (operand reg ay_reg, product reg resa_reg), while the sum it joins sits one further pipeline stage away inside the adder, so folding product and first add into one block needs three stages where the block offers two; and (4) cost without benefit — these multiplies are not on the slowest stage, they pair two to a tile today, and each conversion would take a tile alone, which rule 1 says is paid in tiles for no period gain.

Risks. The main one is the chain-timing reading: I take "chainin … added to the sum at the result register, one clock edge before result" to mean chainin is sampled in the same window in which the product is formed, i.e. the edge that latches result, and chainout to carry the block's registered result. The one-cycle operand skew between A and B follows from exactly that; if the chain input were instead sampled an edge earlier, the pair would need a two-cycle skew, which cannot be built here without raising dot_product_16_8_30_2's latency to 4 — in that case this site must be left alone. Secondary risks: the reset-signal split (A on i_reset, B on r_pipeline_0, requiring the new aclr_pre port on dsp_block_16_8_false, whose only instantiation is at dla_like.small.v:11679), the 70-tile increase, and the fact that the resulting period is set by stages outside this rewrite. No new operation or datatype is introduced.

Read these files in this directory: dla_like.small.v

response

I'll verify the plan against the source.

APPROVE

Verification against the source (/work/dla_like.small.v):

  • Shared reduction is real. Line 11745: resulta_tmp <= ax_reg * ay_reg + bx_reg * by_reg + chainin. The two selected regions ($1166, $1167) are exactly the two products of that single sum — no fabricated terms, no products pulled in from elsewhere (c clear).
  • Two blocks per instance is forced by the mode, not padding. The contract lists registered operands ax, ay, bx only (18 × 19 product plus a 36-bit addend), so one block holds one product; the second product needs a second block with the reduction closed through chainout → chainin. bx = 36'd0 in A and B is the head/chain tie-off pattern, and both blocks carry real operand data with B's result reaching o_result → accumulator_24_30_3. Not a mode island (d clear).
  • Function is preserved bit-exactly. ax/bx are 16-bit unsigned and ay/by 8-bit unsigned (11700–11703), so each product is < 2^24 and fits A's 24-bit result with no intermediate truncation; B's chain add then wraps to 24 bits exactly as the baseline's 64-bit-evaluated RHS does at line 11745. chainin is 64'd0 at the sole instantiation (11687), so it survives as a term on A. (a clear.)
  • Latency and skew check out. Baseline is 3 edges i_features_* → o_result (f_pipeline at e1, operand regs at e2, resulta_tmp at e3). With A fed combinationally from i_features_0/i_weights_0 (deleting f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0, which have no other reader) and B fed from the retained f_pipeline_1_0/w_pipeline_1_0, A's product lands at e2 and is sampled by B's chain input before e3 — both products from the same input cycle, still 3 edges. The reset split (A on i_reset, B on r_pipeline_0 = i_reset delayed, line 11665) reproduces the baseline cycle by cycle: at the de-assert edge B is cleared by r_pipeline_0 so A's one-edge-early clear is unobservable, and at e2 both designs emit 0. ena is indeed dead in the baseline module (declared 11698, unused), so no enable is lost.
  • No new op/datatype. Zero-extension of narrower unsigned operands into the signed ports only (b clear).
  • Winograd deferral is factually grounded. winograd_dsp_16 (8901) has 16-bit resa_reg/resb_reg, so each product is truncated to 16 bits before the downstream add — fusing would not be bit-identical, and resulta/resultb are module outputs with multiple distinct consumers. Leaving them alone is correct, not evasion.

The one-cycle-skew dependence on the chain-sampling edge is disclosed with an explicit fallback, and both the 6.813 ns and 6.808 ns stages are in this module and in the converted set, so the second-slowest twin is removed too.

doing real work, while preserving the design's behaviour and role.
Tier: T2

Hard-block primitive to instantiate: mult_add_int_18x19 (precision INT19)
computes: out = ax*ay + bx
input ports: clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[36], chainin[64], scanin[19]
output ports: result[64], chainout[64], scanout[19]
arithmetic: the block's operands and result are signed two's complement. A design operand narrower than its port must be widened to the port width: zero-extended if the design treats it as unsigned, sign-extended if signed. An unsigned design operand as wide as its port has no exact representation on this block.
timing: result is registered inside the block on clk; it is valid 2 cycles after the input operands are applied.
registers: the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers. Operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register.
reset: reset is synchronous and active high; it clears the block's operand registers and its result register to 0.
tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it, and it is added to the sum at the result register, one clock edge before result); scanin = 0 always (the operand scan chain is not used: ay is driven directly, and scanout is left unconnected); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).

TIER: T2
REGIONS: dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1166, dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1167

BLOCKS: 144
TILES: 184
STAGE_NS: 3.37
MAPPING: per dot_product_16_8_30_2 instance, two chained blocks.
Block A (head): clk=clk; reset=i_reset (new port aclr_pre on dsp_block_16_8_false); ax={2'b0, ax[15:0]} (= i_features_0, unsigned 16 → zero-extend to 18); ay={11'b0, ay[7:0]} (= i_weights_0, unsigned 8 → zero-extend to 19); bx=36'd0; chainin= the module's chainin port (64'd0 at its only instantiation, dla_like.small.v:11687); mode_sigs=12'd0; scanin=19'd0; result unused; chainout → Block B's chainin; scanout unconnected.
Block B (tail): clk=clk; reset=aclr (= r_pipeline_0); ax={2'b0, bx[15:0]} (= f_pipeline_1_0); ay={11'b0, by[7:0]} (= w_pipeline_1_0); bx=36'd0; chainin=Block A's chainout; mode_sigs=12'd0; scanin=19'd0; result[23:0] → resulta (→ o_result), chainout of the module kept as {40'b0, result[23:0]}; scanout unconnected.
PLAN:

What is converted. dsp_block_16_8_false (one definition, 72 instances via dot_product_16_8_30_2) computes one registered sum of two products plus chainin — a genuine common reduction, and it is the design's slowest stage (and its second-slowest, same module, different instance). Both products of every instance are converted: 144 blocks.

Structure and latency. dot_product_16_8_30_2 has three register stages from i_features_* to o_result: f_pipeline_* (reset i_reset), ax_reg/ay_reg/bx_reg/by_reg (reset r_pipeline_0), resulta_tmp (reset r_pipeline_0). Each block supplies two of those stages, so the pair must be skewed by one cycle: Block A is fed combinationally from i_features_0/i_weights_0 — its operand register is f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0, which are deleted — and Block B is fed from the retained f_pipeline_1_0/w_pipeline_1_0. Input before edge e1 → A operands at e1 → A result at e2 → A chainout stable through the window before e3, where B adds it at its result register → o_result after e3. B: input before e1 → f_pipeline_1_0 at e1 → B operands at e2 → B result at e3. Both products come from the same input cycle and the module's latency is 3 edges before and 3 after; ax_reg…by_reg, resulta_tmp and the 24-bit soft adder are deleted. ena is unused in the baseline module, so no enable is lost.

Reset. B's two registers replace ax_reg…by_reg and resulta_tmp, both reset by r_pipeline_0 — an exact match. A's operand register replaces f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0, reset by i_reset, so A's reset is i_reset; that also clears A's result register one edge earlier than the original cleared ax_reg. That difference is unobservable: A's result is consumed only through B's chain input one edge later, and at that edge r_pipeline_0 is high, so B's result register is forced to 0 regardless. I walked the full reset/de-assert sequence (edges e1–e4) and o_result is identical cycle by cycle, including the stale-product value latched at the reset edge itself.

Exactness. ax,bx are 16-bit unsigned and ay,by 8-bit unsigned, each strictly narrower than its signed port, so zero-extension is exact and no value bound is needed (no ASSUMES: line). Each product is < 2^24 and nonnegative; the baseline evaluates the whole RHS in 64 bits (because chainin is 64-bit) and truncates to 24, so it does not truncate per product — the chained blocks also sum at full width and the low 24 bits reproduce the baseline's wrap. chainin is kept as a term on A's chain input, where the rules place a port every instantiation ties to a constant; being constant, its timing alignment is immaterial.

Consumers preserved. resulta → o_result → accumulator_24_30_3.i_result (dla_like.small.v:11494), which only sign-extends and registers it — wire into a register, nothing soft between block and register (rule 2). chainout/chains_0 is dangling at the only instantiation but is still driven identically. f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0 had no reader but the DSP instance, so deleting them hides nothing; i_features_0/i_weights_0 are operands (exempt) and are driven by registers (if_reg_* in the previous processing_element, pipeline0_reg_0 in the weight cache), so no combinational logic lands in front of A.

Delay. Longest series of blocks in any one stage: one. Stages created: register→A/B operands 0.06 + 2.25 = 2.31 (3.88 at the worst sampled wire); A operand reg→A result reg 0.06 + 2.14 = 2.20; A result reg→B result reg over the chain 0.06 + 1.17 + 2.14 = 3.37 (4.42 worst); B result→in_reg 0.06 + 1.48 ≈ 1.6. The 6.813 ns stage (multiply → 12-carry adder chain → LUT → resulta_tmp) and its 6.808 ns twin are both this module and both disappear, as do all 72 siblings of the same shape; the soft 24-bit adder chain and LUT leave the design entirely. The new period is then set by stages I do not touch — the deepest I found are single-level pipelined adds (inverse_winograd_adder_30_3 33-bit, winograd_adder_16_20_4 20-bit) and memory reads, all ≈2–3 ns.

Area. 144 blocks, one tile each (this mode takes its tile alone), displacing the 144 dot-product multiplies that today share 110 of the 114 tiles with the winograd multiplies; the ~80 winograd multiplies left behind repack two to a tile (~40 tiles) → ~184 tiles, upper bound 200 if all 112 winograd multiplies need DSPs, against 306 on the device. DSP area goes 2.89e7 → 4.67e7; cluster area drops slightly (72× a 24-bit adder chain plus ~96 flip-flops each move into the blocks), so total ≈1.85e8, about +11%. ADP improves as long as the new period is below ~6.16 ns, which the eliminated 6.81 ns stages and the ≤4.4 ns worst-case new stages make very likely.

Candidates left alone. All sixteen winograd_dsp_16.$mul$…$733/$734(sum_in=winograd_transform_0/1)#1–#4 regions (112 products) are left, for reasons that apply to all of them together: (1) truncation — resa_reg/resb_reg are 16-bit, so each 16×16 product is truncated to 16 bits before the sum, and winograd_adder_16_20_4 then zero-extends those 16 bits into a 20-bit add; a fused multiply-add truncated after the sum is not bit-identical, which the rules forbid outright; (2) observability — each dsp_out_* leaves winograd_dsp_16 as a module output read by several different winograd_adder_16_20_4 instances and by soft shift/negate expressions (e.g. dsp_out_1_5 feeds WA10 directly, WA20/WA50 negated, WA30 as >>>1, WA40 as -(>>>1)), so the product cannot move inside a block feeding one reduction; (3) latency — the product already uses both of the block's register stages (operand reg ay_reg, product reg resa_reg), while the sum it joins sits one further pipeline stage away inside the adder, so folding product and first add into one block needs three stages where the block offers two; and (4) cost without benefit — these multiplies are not on the slowest stage, they pair two to a tile today, and each conversion would take a tile alone, which rule 1 says is paid in tiles for no period gain.

Risks. The main one is the chain-timing reading: I take "chainin … added to the sum at the result register, one clock edge before result" to mean chainin is sampled in the same window in which the product is formed, i.e. the edge that latches result, and chainout to carry the block's registered result. The one-cycle operand skew between A and B follows from exactly that; if the chain input were instead sampled an edge earlier, the pair would need a two-cycle skew, which cannot be built here without raising dot_product_16_8_30_2's latency to 4 — in that case this site must be left alone. Secondary risks: the reset-signal split (A on i_reset, B on r_pipeline_0, requiring the new aclr_pre port on dsp_block_16_8_false, whose only instantiation is at dla_like.small.v:11679), the 70-tile increase, and the fact that the resulting period is set by stages outside this rewrite. No new operation or datatype is introduced.

);

reg [15:0] ax_reg;
reg [7:0] ay_reg;
reg [15:0] bx_reg;
reg [7:0] by_reg;
reg [23:0] resulta_tmp;
always @(posedge clk) begin
  if(aclr) begin
    resulta_tmp <= 0;
    ax_reg <= 0;
    ay_reg <= 0;
    bx_reg <= 0;
    by_reg <= 0;
  end
  else begin
    ax_reg <= ax;
    ay_reg <= ay;
    bx_reg <= bx;
    by_reg <= by;
    resulta_tmp <= ax_reg * ay_reg + bx_reg * by_reg + chainin;
  end
end
assign resulta = resulta_tmp;
assign chainout = {40'b0, resulta_tmp};


endmodule

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Inner-loop check: syntax + elaboration + cell census. NOT place-and-route.
# usage: bash check.sh <file.v>
# Runs plain yosys (read_verilog; hierarchy; proc; opt; stat) on the one file: no arch, no
# parmys, no hard-block library. Prints one pass/fail line, then the cell census of the whole
# design (the last "Number of cells" table yosys prints): soft arithmetic as $mul/$add/$sub,
# registers as $dff*/$sdff*, memories as $mem*, muxes as $mux/$pmux, and every instantiated
# module by name, which is where a hard-block instantiation such as comb_mult_add_16 shows up
# with its instance count.
set -u
f="${1:?usage: check.sh <file.v>}"
out="$(yosys -p "read_verilog $f; hierarchy -auto-top; proc; opt; stat" 2>&1)"
rc=$?
if [ $rc -ne 0 ]; then
  echo "FAIL: does not synthesize (yosys exit $rc)"
  echo "$out" | grep -iE 'error|warning' | tail -15
  echo "$out" | tail -10
  exit 1
fi
echo "OK: synthesizes cleanly"
echo "Cell census (yosys stat, whole design):"
echo "$out" | awk '/Number of cells:/{buf=$0 "\n"; on=1; next}
                   on && /^[[:space:]]+[$A-Za-z_][^[:space:]]*[[:space:]]+[0-9]+[[:space:]]*$/ {buf=buf $0 "\n"; next}
                   on {on=0}
                   END{printf "%s", buf}' \
  | grep -E 'Number of cells|^[[:space:]]+(\$(mul|add|sub|mem|memrd|memwr|dff|dffe|sdff|sdffe|adff|adffe|mux|pmux)|[A-Za-z_])' || true

You are an INDEPENDENT ACCEPTANCE REVIEWER — the gate of record for whether an adapted design is
a LEGITIMATE member of its benchmark suite. You did NOT plan or write this edit. Judge only what
is in front of you. Your verdict is final and fully automated; there is no human backstop, so err
toward rejecting anything you cannot positively ground.

FRAMING — refute by default. Your job is to find why this adaptation is NOT a legitimate suite
member. Assume it is contrived until the evidence shows otherwise. A rewrite that merely "makes
the mode appear in the netlist" is NOT sufficient — the mode must do real work the original
workload actually implied.

This case has already passed the deterministic structural, provenance, and (for T3) evidence
gates — do not re-litigate those mechanically. Your judgement is the legitimacy call they
cannot make: would a real designer of THIS workload ship this, or is it a mode-island / fabricated
dot-product / gratuitous variant dressed up to pass?

REJECT if any of these hold:

  • (a) FABRICATED TERMS — a fused N-term product/sum aggregates operands that did not genuinely
    share a reduction in the original; the "dot product" is manufactured to fill the mode's arity.
  • (b) MODE ISLAND / DEAD WORK — the mode's result does not flow to the design's real outputs, or
    is wired to constants / a sink that the rest of the design ignores.
  • (c) ROLE BREAK — the adapted region no longer plays the workload role the witness recorded
    (e.g. a control-path multiply repurposed as a fake datapath MAC).
  • (d) UNGROUNDED VARIANT (T3) — the precision/algorithm variant is not the one the cited sibling
    design evidences; the adaptation drifts to a workload character the suite does not contain.

ACCEPT if the adaptation is sound: the mapped arithmetic genuinely existed, the fused terms really
shared a reduction, the mode's output drives the original consumers, and the workload role is
preserved. Legitimate adaptation need not be optimal — only real.

You MUST ground your verdict: cite the specific preserved role (from the witness), the real
region(s)/reduction the mode maps onto, and — for T3 — the sibling evidence axis. A verdict you
cannot ground is a REJECT.

The block's contract, its lines on timing, registers and tie-offs:

I'll examine the file before rendering a verdict.

ACCEPT

Role preserved (witness). The witness records role dsp_block_16_8_false, operation sop, int16 — a two-term signed-magnitude-free MAC inside dot_product_16_8_30_2. After the edit the module is still exactly that: dsp_block_16_8_false (dla_like.small.v:11694) still computes ax*ay + bx*by + chainin, still instantiated once from dot_product_16_8_30_2 (:11676), whose o_result still feeds accumulator_24_30_3 → o_result_* (:11491). No control-path/datapath swap; the datatype and arity are unchanged.

Real reduction mapped, not fabricated (rules out a). The fused terms are the original ones: the witness names the single 24-bit sum ax_reg*ay_reg + bx_reg*by_reg + chainin in resulta_tmp. Block A (:11741) takes ax/ay + the block's own chainin; block B (:11755) takes bx/by and adds A's chainout over the dedicated chain. Each operand pair is the pair the source multiplied; nothing was recruited from an unrelated signal to fill arity. The unused 36-bit addend port is tied to 36'd0 in both blocks — under-filling the mode, not manufacturing a term for it.

Output flows to the real consumers (rules out b). result_b → resulta (:11769) → res → o_result (:11690). result_a_unused dangles only because A's contribution travels the chain instead; A's product is not dead work — it is an addend in B's result register. chainout continues to mirror resulta[23:0] as before (chains_0 was already unconnected pre-edit).

Contract honored. Latency is unchanged at 3 edges from i_features_* to o_result: the deleted f_pipeline_0_0/w_pipeline_0_0 stage is genuinely reabsorbed as A's operand register, A's result register stands in for ax_reg, and B's result register stands in for resulta_tmp — three registers before and after, so done_5/valid_* alignment in the parent still holds. Term alignment is correct: chain_a_to_b settles one edge before B's result edge, matching the contract's "chainin … added at the result register, one clock edge before result", and both terms carry the same input sample. Tie-offs comply: scanin = 0, mode_sigs = 0, scanout unconnected, and A's chainin traces to the constant 64'd0 at :11685. Zero-extension of unsigned 16/8-bit operands into the signed 18/19-bit ports is exact, and the 64-bit sum truncated at [23:0] reproduces the original 24-bit wraparound.

Minor, non-disqualifying. The single reset port forces A's result register onto aclr_pre (i_reset) rather than the one-cycle-later r_pipeline_0 that governed ax_reg. Tracing an assert/deassert shows the divergence is flushed by B's aclr before it can reach resulta, so the output behavior matches; it is a retiming artifact of the primitive's single reset, not a contrivance.

T2, no variant introduced, so no sibling-evidence axis applies and (d) is not in play.

output ports: result[64], chainout[64], scanout[19]
arithmetic: the block's operands and result are signed two's complement. A design operand narrower than its port must be widened to the port width: zero-extended if the design treats it as unsigned, sign-extended if signed. An unsigned design operand as wide as its port has no exact representation on this block.
timing: result is registered inside the block on clk; it is valid 2 cycles after the input operands are applied.
registers: the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers. Operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register.
reset: reset is synchronous and active high; it clears the block's operand registers and its result register to 0.

An id names a product by its module and its multiply. One that ends in (sum_in=<module>) is a product whose sum sits in that other module: the product leaves its own module through a port and is added there, so the arithmetic to convert spans both. A #1, #2 after an id separates instances of the same product that sit in sums of different shapes.

Baseline: the unmodified dla_like.small under this run's VPR settings (regime 'koios_small': channel width 300, device koios_small, seed 1) has a critical path of 6.81328 ns and uses 114 DSP tiles of the device's 306, one block per tile. Its used logic-block area is 1.67592e+08 minimum-width transistor areas (one DSP tile 253779, one cluster 27905), so its area-delay product is 1.14185e+09. Its slowest stage is in dsp_block_16_8_false: one hard multiply on a DSP tile (two_mult_18x19), then one adder chain of 12 carry stages, then one LUT, into a flip-flop with synchronous clear (resulta_tmp); wire 1.73 ns into the multiply, 0.96 into the adder, 1.39 into the LUT. The next slowest stage, in a different dsp_block_16_8_false instance (endpoint group $sdff~5296, not path 1's $sdff~4939), is the same shape as path 1 — one hard multiply on a DSP tile, one adder chain of 15 carry stages, one LUT, into the resulta_tmp flip-flop, 6.808 ns, so shortening the slowest stage past that point buys nothing on its own. Any path your rewrite creates is compared against that critical path.

Rewrite rules

A stage is the logic between one register and the next. A block is one instance of the target mode. These rules hold for any synchronous design on any FPGA. Numbers that depend on this architecture are in the last section; use them, do not restate them as rules.

Must hold

Going against these produces a wrong design, not a slower one.

Observability. Only a block's output is visible. A value read by anything other than the arithmetic being taken into the block (another expression, a module output port, another module) cannot go inside a block. Keep it outside, or recompute it for those readers. Check the source; detection does not see port consumers. Operands are exempt: the block takes a copy and the original signal still exists.

Register control. A register carries its reset, enable and initial value. These modes hold no registers, so taking a register inside deletes that behaviour. If anything depends on it, keep the register and drive it from the block's result, or leave the site alone.

Arithmetic. The block computes the mode's expression on signed ports. Match the original bit for bit or leave the site alone. Keep every term of the original expression, including ports every instantiation ties to a constant. A term that goes on no block port (an addend beside the products) stays outside the block, added to its result; the flow flattens the design and removes an add of a constant, so such a term puts nothing on the stage and rule 2 does not count it. A bound on a port's values may be used only if the plan states it and names its source in the design, on a line of its own beginning ASSUMES:; such a rewrite is recorded as verified under that assumption. Fold a term only when it is constant inside the module itself, and say so in the plan.

design operandon a signed portallowed
unsigned, narrower than the portzero-extendyes
unsigned, as wide as the port, range provably below the signed maximum (constant coefficient, bounded value)zero-extend, state the bound and where it comes fromyes
unsigned, as wide as the port, range not boundable from the source (a top-level input)no exact representationno
original truncates each product before the suma fused sum truncated after differsno
original's narrow sum register wrapsthe low bits of the wider result reproduce the wrapyes
Weigh

The clock period is the delay of the slowest stage in the whole design. A stage's delay is the sum of everything on it: each block's fixed delay, each wire, each soft cell. Each relationship below names what it does to delay and to area. If a plan goes against one, say why.

1. Slowest stage → all outputs

Your task names the design's slowest stage and what is on it.

Slowest stage → delay

Relationship: A rewrite that does not touch the slowest stage cannot make the design faster, and must leave every stage no slower than it was. A rewrite that does touch it must make it shorter, not merely different. Compare every stage you change against it.

Slowest stage → area

Relationship: A rewrite that cannot shorten the clock period is paid for in tiles alone. Say so in the plan.

2. Block to register → delay

Relationship: A block's result goes to a register, or into the next block, with nothing but wire between. Any soft arithmetic between a block and its register is added to the stage.

3. Blocks in series → all outputs
Blocks in series → delay

Relationship: Each block in series adds its own delay plus one wire. Sum them with the wire in and the wire out, and compare against the slowest stage. That comparison, not a fixed count, says how many fit.

Blocks in series → area

Relationship: None. Series or side by side, the same blocks take the same tiles.

4. Arithmetic inside the block → all outputs

Arithmetic inside the block is what the mode's expression computes rather than what sits in series with it: a multiply and one addend, or two products and their sum, depending on the mode.

Arithmetic inside the block → delay

Relationship: Free. The block's delay is the mode's own and does not rise because its adder is used. This is where a conversion wins: soft logic leaves the stage and the block's delay already covers the work. An adder feeding the block's addend port is mapped into the block; an adder reading the block's result is on the path after it (rule 2). A block whose addend port is tied to a constant takes in nothing; if the multiply it replaces is already on a tile, the conversion changes the tile count and nothing else.

Arithmetic inside the block → area

Relationship: Flip-flops fall where product registers go. Do not count on LUT savings.

5. Registers → all outputs
Registers → delay

Relationship: A register may move only if no stage becomes longer and the module's latency is unchanged. If one must be added to keep latency, put it where it splits the longest stage. Every other operand entering that stage must be delayed by the same amount, or the stage adds values from different cycles.

Registers → area

Relationship: A register placed mid-chain costs operand-delay registers that a register after the chain does not.

6. Tiles → area

Relationship: A block occupies one DSP tile in one mode. Do not assume the multiply it replaces frees a tile: the synthesis tool may already have placed that multiply on a tile shared with others. Count the blocks against the tiles the unmodified design uses and the device's count, both in your task.

Every DSP mode costs the same 2.14 ns. The invented modes have no chain ports, so a partial sum passes between blocks through general routing. The packer can place up to four multiplies of 9 bits or less on one tile and up to two of 18 bits or less; a block in mult_add_mode_18_19_36 takes its tile alone.

Before you commit to a plan

State in your plan: how many blocks and how many tiles on top of the unmodified design's; the longest series of blocks in any stage and its delay from the table, against the slowest stage; and what soft logic leaves the stage you touch, and whether that stage is the slowest one. If a site does not fit under those numbers, arrange it differently, take fewer, or leave it, and name the rule that ruled it out.

This mode's block holds registers

The rules above were written for blocks with no registers. This run's target mode is a registered block: its contract states a latency in cycles, where its registers sit, what its reset does and which ports are tied off. Read the rules above with the changes below.

Must hold

Register control, for this mode. The block has two register stages of its own: one on the operands, before the products, and one on the sum. A design register may go inside the block only as one of those two stages. A register on the operands, or on each product before the sum, is the first stage; the register that holds the sum is the second. The block's registers have the reset the contract states, no enable and no other initial value. A design register that has an enable, a different reset behaviour, or a reset signal other than the one you connect to the block's reset port cannot be replaced by the block's register: keep it outside or leave the site alone. Connect the block's reset to the signal that reset the registers it replaces, or to 0 if they had none.

Latency (rule 5, and it must hold). The module's latency must be unchanged: every output carries the same value on the same cycle as before. Count the register stages the original has from the operands to the register that holds the sum, and compare with the block's latency.

the original hasthen
as many stages as the blockthe block's registers stand in place of the design's; remove exactly those and move nothing else
morethe extra stage stays outside the block, on the operands before it or on the result after it, where it was
fewerthe block adds the difference: move a register that already exists on the same path into the block's place, or delay every other value that meets the block's result by the same number of cycles so that the stage adds values from the same cycle; if neither can be done without changing when the module's outputs change, leave the site alone

State in the plan the stages the original has around each site, which of them the block replaces, and the module's latency before and after. They must be equal.

Addends and the chain input. A term the original adds to the products before the sum's register cannot be added to the block's result on the same cycle: the result is already registered. The chain input is a dedicated pin that takes only another block's chain output or a constant. If the term is a module port that every instantiation ties to a constant, connect that port to the chain input: the block is then tied off exactly where the original was, and the module still computes what it computed for every value of its ports. Any other addend needs its own register (same reset) and an add after the block, which rule 2 counts on the next stage.

Weigh

Stages (rules 2 and 3). A registered block ends the stage that reaches its operand ports and starts a new one at its result. The wire into the block and the wire out of it are on different stages. Soft arithmetic after the block is on the stage after the block's result register, not in series with the block's delay. Between its own two registers the block takes its fixed delay from the table.

Flip-flops (rule 4). The design registers the block replaces leave the soft logic.

Objective: minimize the design's area-delay product, VPR's used area times the critical path, against the baseline in your task. A rewrite that does not lower it is not worth making; say so and output TIER: NONE rather than convert sites for their own sake. Among rewrites that lower it, prefer the one that lowers it most.

Classify the tier of the change you propose (a datatype/algorithm variant, T3, is out of scope —
do not propose one):

  • T1: Direct replacement: no change to the computed function or surrounding structure; no datatype change.
  • T2: Local restructuring only; preserve the region's function, role, and live consumers; no new operation/datatype family.

response

I'll read the source and the memory index first.

TIER: T2
REGIONS: dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1166, dsp_block_16_8_false.$mul$dla_like.small.v:11745$1167
SHARED_REDUCTION: dsp_block_16_8_false.resulta_tmp (the 24-bit sum ax_reg*ay_reg + bx_reg*by_reg + chainin, read out as resulta)
BLOCKS: 72
TILES: 150
STAGE_NS: 5.62
MAPPING: per dsp_block_16_8_false instance, one mult_add_int_18x19:
clk ← clk; reset ← aclr (the only reset of ax_reg/ay_reg/bx_reg/by_reg/resulta_tmp, synchronous, active high, clears to 0; the module's ena port is unused in the baseline body, so no enable is lost); mode_sigs ← 12'b0; scanin ← 19'b0, scanout left unconnected.
ax[17:0] ← {2'b0, ax[15:0]} (region $1166's first operand, 16-bit unsigned, zero-extended, max 65535 < 2^17).
ay[18:0] ← {11'b0, ay[7:0]} (region $1166's second operand, 8-bit unsigned, zero-extended).
bx[35:0] ← {12'b0, prod_b[23:0]} where wire [23:0] prod_b = bx[15:0] * by[7:0]; — region $1167's product, left as a plain * so it maps to a hard 18x19 multiply (two per tile, exactly as in the baseline), combinational into the block's addend port; unsigned, max 16,711,425 < 2^24, zero-extended.
chainin[63:0] ← the module's chainin port, which the single instantiation (dot_product_16_8_30_2, line 11687) ties to 64'd0 — a constant driver, the only kind this dedicated pin accepts, and it is the term the baseline adds at the result register.
result[63:0] → assign resulta = result[23:0]; and assign chainout = {40'b0, result[23:0]}; (wires and constants only).

PLAN:

What the block computes, and why it is bit-exact. The baseline module (lines 11727–11749) registers ax,ay,bx,by at edge k and registers ax_reg*ay_reg + bx_reg*by_reg + chainin into the 24-bit resulta_tmp at edge k+1. The block's own two register stages stand exactly in place of those: its operand register replaces ax_reg, ay_reg (driven directly) and replaces bx_reg/by_reg by registering their product instead of their operands — equivalent because the multiply is combinational, so the value registered on bx at edge k is bx@k * by@k, and because aclr clearing bx_reg/by_reg to 0 and clearing the bx port register to 0 both contribute 0 to the sum. Its result register replaces resulta_tmp. Module latency is 2 cycles before and 2 cycles after; nothing else moves. All operands are unsigned and narrower than their signed ports, so zero-extension is exact; the full sum ax*ay + bx*by + chainin can exceed 2^24 and the baseline's 24-bit register wraps — taking result[23:0] reproduces that wrap bit for bit, and truncation commutes with the +chainin term, so correctness does not depend on chainin being 0. Nothing is truncated before the sum in this region, so the fusion is legal here.

Live values preserved. resulta_tmp is read only by resulta and chainout; ax_reg…by_reg are read only by the sum. resulta drives o_result → DP_res_* → accumulator_24_30_3.i_result, which sign-extends bit 23 — untouched, since resulta carries the same bits on the same cycles. chainout is rebuilt as {40'b0, result[23:0]} rather than taken from the block's chainout pin, so its upper 40 bits stay 0 as the baseline has them (chains_0 in dot_product_16_8_30_2 is declared and never read, but the port is preserved anyway). No new operation or datatype family: the only cells added are the block and the wiring; the bx*by multiply is the baseline's own expression, unmoved.

Blocks, tiles, stages. 72 blocks (one per dsp_block_16_8_false instance; the module is instantiated once in dot_product_16_8_30_2, 6 per processing_element, 72 in all), each alone on a tile. The 72 surviving bx*by multiplies pack two to a tile as they do today, ≈36 tiles, and the ≈84 remaining multiplies elsewhere keep the ≈42 tiles the previous build measured: ≈150 tiles, +36 over the baseline's 114, of the device's 306. (The previous build's tile count came in 2 above the estimate; expect ±2 here for the same packing reason.) No stage carries two block delays: the mult_add is a registered block, so its bx input pin ends the stage that the combinational multiply sits on, and its own 2.14 ns runs between its two internal registers. The stage I create is register → wire → hard multiply (2.14) → wire → block operand pin: 0.06 + 2.25 + 2.14 + 1.17 = 5.62 ns with table medians, and 5.10 ns using the 1.73 ns register-to-multiply wire the baseline timing report measured in this very region — below the baseline's 6.81 ns slowest stage either way.

What leaves the slowest stage. The baseline's slowest stage is exactly this region: multiply → 12-carry-stage soft adder (0.96) → LUT (1.39) → resulta_tmp. The adder chain, the LUT and the +chainin add all move inside the block, where rule 4 makes them free. This is the design's slowest stage and its second-slowest (same shape, a different instance of the same module), and since every one of the 72 instances is that same shape, all 72 must be converted — converting a subset would leave 6.81 ns standing and buy only tiles. The previous build's 5.98771 ns path ran from a flip-flop through four LUT levels into a soft ALU bit inside a converted dsp_block (…cell_hard_block$27364.…replace_alu$57355.Y[26]) and straight on into the accumulator's carry chain: that soft 24-bit adder was the price of putting the two products in two separate blocks and summing them outside. This plan has no soft arithmetic left in dsp_block_16_8_false at all, so that path disappears, 72 blocks and ~36 tiles are saved against the previous build, and the expected product is ≈1.68e+08 × ≈5.6–6.0 ns ≈ 0.94–1.01e+09, i.e. −12% to −18% against the baseline's 1.14185e+09 and below the kept 1.0693e+09 on area alone even if the period only matches 5.99 ns.

Candidates left, and why. All 16 winograd_dsp_16 candidates (both $733/$734 products, both sum_in=winograd_transform_0 and =winograd_transform_1, all #1–#4 shapes) are left, for one shared set of reasons: (a) each product is truncated into a 16-bit register (resa_reg <= ay_reg * coefa, lines 8966–8967) and the parent's winograd_adder_16_20_4 tree zero-extends that truncated 16-bit value into 20-bit adds — the design truncates each product before the sum, which the table forbids fusing; (b) the coefficient operand is a 16-bit register holding a negative constant encoded unsigned (−5 as 65531, etc.), so on a signed 19-bit port only the low 16 bits of the product agree with the design, which is the same truncation requirement again; (c) the products leave their module on resulta/resultb and several feed more than one reduction (dsp_out_1_4 goes to both winograd_adder_16_20_4_WA00.data7x and …_WA10.data10x), so they feed separate sums and are observable at ports; (d) their sums sit three pipeline levels deep in another module, so fusing would change where registers sit across a module boundary. On top of that, they are not on the slowest stage, so blocks spent there would be paid for in tiles alone (≈9 CLBs each) with no period to show for it.

Risks. (1) The surviving bx * by must still be inferred onto a DSP tile; it is the baseline's own expression with the same widths in the same module, but if the flow mapped it to soft logic the new stage would blow past 6.81 ns. (2) The register-to-multiply and multiply-to-block wires are the sampled ranges in the table; at their high ends (3.82 + 2.22) this stage would reach 8.2 ns, though the baseline measured 1.73 ns on the first of them in this exact region. (3) The period may be floored by a soft path outside this region near 6 ns, in which case the gain is the 36 tiles and the removed soft adder rather than more speed — still a lower product than the kept result.

 "content": "---\nname: dla-mult-add-fusion-shape\ndescription: On dla_like.small, fusing dsp_block_16_8_false with a mult-add hard block is cheapest when the second product enters through the bx addend port from a plain combinational multiply\nmetadata:\n  type: project\n---\n\nIn the dla_like.small hard-block planning runs (run of 2026-10-06, regime koios_small,\nbaseline 6.81328 ns / 1.67592e+08 area / 114 DSP tiles), `dsp_block_16_8_false` computes\n`resulta_tmp <= ax_reg*ay_reg + bx_reg*by_reg + chainin` in one 24-bit result register.\n\nTwo ways to map it onto a one-multiply mult-add mode (`out = ax*ay + bx`):\n- two blocks, one per product, plus a soft 24-bit adder after them: 144 blocks, 186 tiles,\n  measured 5.98771 ns, product 1.0693e+09 (-6.35%). The soft adder goes combinational into\n  `accumulator_24_30_3`'s add and lands on the critical path.\n- one block per instance, with `bx*by` left as a plain `*` (hard 18x19, packs 2 per tile)\n  driving the block's 36-bit `bx` addend port: 72 blocks, ~150 tiles, the shared reduction\n  happens inside the block and no soft adder remains.\n\n**Why:** the block's operand register sits before its multiplier, so a combinational product\nformed from the module inputs and registered on the `bx` port is bit-exact with the design's\n`bx_reg*by_reg` and keeps latency at 2 cycles; 36 of the 72 extra tiles disappear because the\nleftover multiplies still pair two to a tile.\n\n**How to apply:** for a one-multiply mult-add mode on a two-product MAC, check whether one\nproduct can ride the addend port from a surviving hard multiply before spending a second block\non it. Any path your rewrite creates is compared against that critical path.
The block's contract, its lines on timing, registers and tie-offs:
timing: result is registered inside the block on clk; it is valid 2 cycles after the input operands are applied.
registers: the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers. Operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register.
tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it, and it is added to the sum at the result register, one clock edge before result); scanin = 0 always (the operand scan chain is not used: ay is driven directly, and scanout is left unconnected); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
